Diagonals and Block-Ordered Relations
Roland Backhouse, Ed Voermans

Abstract
More than 70 years ago, Jaques Riguet suggested the existence of an “analogie frappante” (striking analogy) between so-called “relations de Ferrers” and a class of difunctional relations, members of which we call “diagonals”. Inspired by his suggestion, we formulate an “analogie frappante” linking the notion of a block-ordered relation and the notion of the diagonal of a relation. We formulate several novel properties of the core/index of a diagonal, and use these properties to rephrase our “analogie frappante”. Loosely speaking, we show that a block-ordered relation is a provisional ordering up to isomorphism and reduction to its core. (Our theorems make this informal statement precise.) Unlike Riguet (and others who follow his example), we avoid almost entirely the use of nested complements to express and reason about properties of these notions: we use factors (aka residuals) instead. The only (and inevitable) exception to this is to show that our definition of a “staircase” relation is equivalent to Riguet’s definition of a “relation de Ferrers”. Our “analogie frappante” also makes it obvious that a “staircase” relation is not necessarily block-ordered, in spite of the mental picture of such a relation presented by Riguet.



1
Introduction

More than seventy years ago, in a series of publications
[
Rig48
,
Rig50
,
Rig51
]
, Jacques Riguet introduced
the notions of a “relation difonctionelle” and “relations de Ferrers”. In

[
Rig51
]
he remarked on an “analogie frappante” between these two notions via what he referred to
as the “différence” of a given relation. Riguet
[
Rig51
]
states the following theorem:

Pour que
R
soit une relation de Ferrers, il
faut et il suffit que
R
soit réunion de rectangles dont les projections de même nom sont totalement
ordonnées par inclusion et tels que si la première projection de l’un des rectangles est contenue dans
la première projection d’un autre rectangle, la seconde projection du second est contenue dans
la seconde projection du premier.

For those unable to read French, the theorem states a necessary and sufficient condition for a relation to
be “de Ferrers” in terms of totally ordered rectangles (“rectangles
\ldots
totalement
ordonnées”). The theorem clearly begs the question what is the definition of a “relation de Ferrers”. We
postpone answering this question until section
7
. The reason for doing so is that Riguet gives both a formal
definition and a mental picture —a picture like the one in fig.
1
of what we call a “staircase
relation”— but it is far from obvious how Riguet’s definition and the mental picture are related.

Figure 1:
Mental Picture of a Staircase Relation

Riguet does not give a proof of the theorem and his “analogie frappante” between “relations de Ferrers” and
difunctional relations is unclear. The proof of Riguet’s theorem is quite
straightforward: see
[
Bac21
]
. However, clarifying the “analogie frappante” is more difficult.

The work presented here initially began as an attempt to properly understand Riguet’s work and to
rectify errors in extant literature. We introduce, in section
5
, the “diagonal” of a
relation and, in section
6
, the notion of a “block-ordered relation”. The
“diagonal” of a relation is what Riguet referred to as the “différence”; it is a difunctional relation. We
formulate an “analogie frappante” (specifically, theorem
114
) linking block-ordered relations
and diagonals. We also formalise the notion of a “staircase” relation: as shown in
[
Bac21
]
, our
definition of a ‘‘staircase’’ relation and Riguet’s definition of a ‘‘relation de Ferrers’’ are
equivalent
1
1

1

The name “staircase” is more informative than “relation de Ferrers” and our
definition uses factors rather than complementation (from which Riguet’s terminology “différence” is
derived), which is why we prefer to deviate from Riguet’s presentation
. Ostensibly a “staircase”
relation is “block-ordered” where the “blocks” are totally ordered. But, as we observed in our
initial investigations, this is not the case: as we show in section
7
, the less-than
relation on real numbers is a “staircase” relation but is not “block-ordered”.

In addition to our “analogie frappante”, a major contribution of our work is the application of the notions
of a core/index of a relation. These notions and their properties are briefly summarised in section

4
. Informally, a core
2
2

2

We say “a” core because a relation typically has many
cores; all cores of a relation are, however, isomorphic.
of a relation captures its essential properties. For example, a core
of a difunctional relation is an isomorphism. An index of a relation is a core of the relation that has the
same type as the relation.
A fuller account of their properties is given in
[
BV22
,
BV23
]
. In this paper, we
state and prove a number of properties of the core/index of the diagonal of a relation. These properties
are relevant to our analogie frappante: theorem
114
gives a method of testing whether or not a
relation is block-ordered in terms of the diagonal of the relation, and
theorem
116
reformulates the test in terms of the core of the relation.

During the course of our investigation, we became aware of very similar work by Winter
[
Win04
]
.
Winter’s notion of “relations of order shape” is identical to our notion of “block-ordered relation”.
Winter also introduces the notion of the “diagonal” of a relation — but does not give the notion a name
and does not attribute the notion to Riguet
[
Rig51
]
. (He does cite
[
Rig51
]
and he denotes the
“diagonal” of a relation
R
by
R^{d}
, his definition of which is identical to the definition of the “différence” of
R

given by Riguet.) Consequently, there is some overlap between our work and Winter’s work. We believe
the overlap is justified because we formulate the notion of a “diagonal” in terms of factors (“residuals” in
the terminology used by Winter) and we avoid the use of complements altogether — with the exception
of section
7
where we observe the equivalence of the notion of a “staircase”
relation with Riguet’s notion of a “relation de Ferrers”. (Winter does express some properties in terms of
factors but, following in Riguet’s footsteps, his calculations invariably use complements, which he
denotes by an overbar.) Winter also observes that not all “staircase” relations are “block-ordered” but
does not give any concrete example. See the concluding section for further discussion of this aspect of
Winter’s paper.

In order to make this paper relatively self-contained, sections
2
and
3

summarise the axioms of (point-free) relation algebra and some basic concepts. Typically, proofs of
properties stated in these sections are omitted. Exceptions to this rule concern properties that we deem
less familiar to many readers (for example, factorisation properties of functional relations in section

3.4
). Section
4
introduces the notions of a core/index of a relation. For proofs
of properties stated in this section see
[
BV22
,
BV23
]
.

2
Axioms of Point-free Relation Algebra

In this section, we give a brief summary of the axioms of point-free relation algebra. For full details
of the axioms, see
[
BDGv22
]
.

2.1
Summary

Point-free relation algebra comprises three layers with interfaces between the layers plus additional axioms
peculiar to relations. The axiom system is typed. For types
A
and
B
,
A{\sim}B
denotes a set; the elements of the set are called

(heterogeneous) relations of type

A{\sim}B
. Elements of type
A{\sim}A
, for some type
A
, are called
homogeneous
relations
.

The first layer axiomatises the properties of a partially ordered set. We postulate that, for each pair of
types
A
and
B
,
A{\sim}B
forms a complete, universally distributive lattice. We use the symbol “
{\subseteq}
” for the ordering relation, and “
{\cup}
”
and “
{\cap}
” for the supremum and infimum operators. We assume that this notation is familiar to the
reader, allowing us to skip a more detailed account of its properties. However, we use
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

for the least element of the ordering (rather than the conventional
\emptyset
) and
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
for the greatest element. In keeping with the
conventional practice of overloading the symbol “
\emptyset
”, both these symbols are overloaded.

It is important to note that there is no axiom stating that a relation is a set, and there is no corresponding
notion of membership. The lack of a notion of membership distinguishes point-free relation algebra from
pointwise algebra.

The second layer adds a composition operator. If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type

B{\sim}C
, the composition of
R
and
S
is a relation of type
A{\sim}C
which we denote by
R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S
. Composition is
associative and, for each type
A
, there is an identity relation which we denote by
I_{A}
. We often overload
the notation for the identity relation, writing just
I
. Occasionally, for greater clarity, we do supply the
type information.

The interface between the first and second layers defines a relation algebra to be an instance of a
regular
algebra

[
Bac06
]
(also called a
standard Kleene algebra
, or
S
-
algebra

[
Con71
]
). For this paper,
the most important aspect of this interface is the existence and properties of the factor operators. These
are introduced in section
2.2
. Also,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
is a zero of composition: for all
R
,

{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.

The completeness axiom in the first layer allows the reflexive-transitive closure
R^{*}
of
each element
R
of type
A{\sim}A
, for some type
A
, to be defined.

The third layer is the introduction of a
converse
operator. If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
, the converse of

R
, which we denote by
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
(pronounced
R
“wok”) is a relation of type
B{\sim}A
.
The interface with the first layer is that converse is simultaneously the lower and upper adjoint in a Galois
connection, and thus a poset isomorphism (in particular,
{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
and

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
); the interface with the
second layer is formed by the two rules
I^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptI
and, for all relations
R
and
S
of appropriate type,

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.

Additional axioms characterise properties peculiar to relations.
The modularity rule (aka Dedekind’s rule
[
Rig48
]
) is that, for all relations
R
,
S
and
T
,

(1)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT)~~.

The dual property, obtained by exploiting properties of the converse operator, is, for all relations
R
,
S
and
T
,

(2)

S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

The modularity rule is
necessary to the derivation of some of the properties we state without proof (for example, the
properties of the domain operators given in section
3.2
). Another rule is the
cone rule
:

(3)

{\left\langle\forall{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{:}{:}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\right\rangle}~~.

The cone rule limits consideration to “unary” relation algebras: constructing the cartesian product of two
relation algebras to form a relation algebra (whereby the operators are defined pointwise) does not yield
an algebra satisfying the cone rule.

2.2
Factors

If
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type
A{\sim}C
, the relation
R{\setminus}S
of type
B{\sim}C
is defined by
the Galois connection, for all
T
(of type
B{\sim}C
),

(4)

T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptS~~.

Similarly, if
R
is a relation of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
is a relation of type
C{\sim}B
, the relation
R{/}S
of type
A{\sim}C
is defined by
the Galois connection, for all
T
,

(5)

T\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{/}S\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR~~.

The existence of factors is a property of a regular algebra; in relation algebra, factors are also known as
“residuals”. Factors enjoy a rich theory which underlies many of our calculations.

The relations
R{\setminus}R
(of type
B{\sim}B
if
R
has type
A{\sim}B
) and
R{/}R
(of type
A{\sim}A
if
R
has type
A{\sim}B
) play a
central rôle in section
7
. As is easily verified, both are
preorders
. That is, both are
transitive
:

R{\setminus}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 13.49945pt{\wedge}\hskip 13.49945ptR{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{/}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R

and both are
reflexive
:

I\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951ptI\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{/}R~~.

(The notation “
I
” is overloaded in the above equation. In the left conjunct, it
denotes the identity relation of type
B{\sim}B
and, in the right conjunct, it
denotes the identity relation of type
A{\sim}A
, assuming
R
has type
A{\sim}B
. We often overload constants in
this way. Note, however, that we do not attempt to combine the two inclusions into one.)
In addition, for all
R
,

(6)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptR{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(7)

R{/}(R{\setminus}R)\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{/}R){\setminus}R~~,

(8)

(R{\setminus}R){/}(R{\setminus}R)\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\setminus}R){\setminus}(R{\setminus}R)\mbox{~~and}

(9)

(R{/}R){\setminus}(R{/}R)\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{/}R){/}(R{/}R)~~.

In fact, we don’t use (
7
) directly; its relevance is as the initial step in proving the leftmost
equations of (
8
) and (
9
). We choose not to exploit the associativity of the over and
under operators in (
8
) and (
9
) —by writing, for example,
(R{\setminus}R){/}(R{\setminus}R)
as

R{\setminus}R{/}(R{\setminus}R)
— in order to emphasise their rôle as properties of the
preorders
R{\setminus}R
and
R{/}R
.

In relation algebra (as opposed to regular algebra) it is possible to eliminate the factor operators
altogether because they can be expressed in terms of complements and converse. The rules for doing so
are as follows: for all
R
,
S
and
T
,

(10)

R{\setminus}S\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}S)\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptS{/}T\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}({\neg}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

(11)

R{\setminus}S{/}T\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

Although the elimination of factors is highly
undesirable, we are obliged to introduce complements in order to compare our work with that of Riguet
in section
7
on staircase relations.

Property (
6
) exemplifies how much easier calculations with factors can be compared to
calculations that combine complements with converses. The property is very easy to spot and apply.
Expressed using (
11
), it is equivalent to

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R)\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt{\neg}({\neg}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

In this form, the property is difficult to spot and its correct application is difficult to check.

It is useful to record the distributivity properties of converse over the factor operators:

Lemma 12

For all
R
and
S
,

(13)

R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(S{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}R\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}S~~.

Symmetrically,

(14)

R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(S{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}S~~.

Also,

(15)

(R{\setminus}S{/}T)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

\Box

In (
13
) and (
14
), the inclusion of terms involving complements
is only relevant in section
7
.

3
Some Definitions

This section introduces a number of concepts which have been studied in detail elsewhere. For the most
part, their properties are stated without proof. An exception to this is in section
3.4
where we
combine the Galois connections defining factors with the Galois connection defining functionality.

3.1
Coreflexives

In point-free relation algebra, “coreflexives” of a given type represent sets of elements of that type. A

coreflexive of type

A
is a relation
p
such that
p\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptI_{A}
. Frequently used properties are that, for all
coreflexives
p
,

p\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p

and, for all coreflexives
p
and
q
,

p{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}q\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptq\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptq{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p~~.

(The proof of these properties relies on the modularity rule.) In the literature, coreflexives have several
different names, usually depending on the application area in question. Examples are “monotype”,
“pid” (short for “partial identity”) and “test”.

3.2
The Domain Operators

The “domain operators” (see eg.
[
BH93
]
) play a dominant and unavoidable role. We
exploit their properties frequently in calculations, so much so that we assume great familiarity with
them.

Definition 16
(Domain Operators)

Given relation
R
of type
A{\sim}B
,
the
left domain

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
of
R
is a relation of type
A
defined by the equation

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptI_{A}\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

and the
right domain

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
of
R
is a relation of type
B
is defined by the equation

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptI_{B}\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

\Box

The name “domain operator” is chosen because of the fundamental properties: for all
R
and all
coreflexives
p
,

(17)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}p\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptp

and

(18)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptp{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptp\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

A simple, often-used consequence of (
17
) and (
18
) is the property:

(19)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

As is the case for factors, the domain operators enjoy a rich theory which underlies many of our
calculations but we omit the details here.

3.3
Pers and Per Domains

Given relations
R
of type
A{\sim}B
and
S
of type
A{\sim}C
, the symmetric

right-division
is the relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}S
of type
B{\sim}C
defined in terms of
right
factors as

(20)

R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}S\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(S{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Dually, given relations
R
of type
B{\sim}A
and
S
of type
C{\sim}A
, the
symmetric
left-division
is the relation
R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}S
of type
B{\sim}C
defined in terms of left factors as

(21)

R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}S\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{/}S\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982pt(S{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

The relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R
is an equivalence relation.
Voermans
[
Voe99
]
calls it the “greatest
right domain” of
R
. Riguet
[
Rig48
]
calls
R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R
the “noyau” of
R
(but defines it using nested
complements). Others (see
[
Oli18
]
for references) call it the “kernel” of
R
.

As remarked elsewhere
[
Oli18
]
, the
symmetric left-division
inherits a number of (left) cancellation
properties from the properties of factorisation in terms of which it is defined. In this section the focus is on
the left and right “per domains” introduced by Voermans
[
Voe99
]
.

Definition 22
(Right and Left Per Domains)

The
right per domain
of relation
R
, denoted

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
, is defined by the equation

(23)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R~~.

Dually, the
left per domain
of relation
R
, denoted
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
, is defined by the equation

(24)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

\Box

In order to prove additional
properties, it is useful to record the left and right domains of the relation
R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.

Lemma 25

For all
R
,

(R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R~~.

\Box

The left and right per domains are “pers” where “per” is an abbreviation of “partial equivalence
relation”.

Definition 26
(Partial Equivalence Relation (per))

A relation is a
partial equivalence
relation
iff it is symmetric and transitive. That is,
R
is a partial equivalence relation iff

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Henceforth we abbreviate partial equivalence relation to
per
.

\Box

That
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
are indeed pers is a direct consequence of the symmetry and transitivity of
R{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R
.

The left and right per domains are called “domains” because, like the coreflexive domains, we have the
properties: for all relations
R
and pers
P
,

(27)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptP\mbox{~~, and}

(28)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptP{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptP\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}~~.

The right per domain
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
can be defined equivalently by the equation

(29)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}{\hskip-4.2679pt}{\setminus}{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Moreover,

(30)

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

(See
[
Bac21
]
for the proofs of these properties.) Symmetrical properties hold of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
.

For further properties of pers and per domains, see
[
Voe99
]
.

3.4
Functionality

In this section, we present a number of lesser-known properties of “functional” relations. A relation
R
of type
A{\sim}B

is said to be
left
-
functional
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
. Equivalently,
R
is
left-functional
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptI_{A}
.
It is said to be
right-functional
iff
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
(equivalently,
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptI_{B}
). A relation
R

is said to be a
bijection
iff it is both left- and right-functional.

Rather than left- and right-functional, the more common terminology is “functional” and “injective”
but publications differ on which of left- or right-functional is “functional” or “injective”. We choose to
abbreviate “left-functional” to
functional
and to use the term
injective
instead of right-functional.
Typically, we use
f
and
g
to denote functional relations, and Greek letters to denote bijections (although
the latter is not always the case). Other authors make the opposite choice.

The properties we present here stem from
the observation that functionality can be defined via a Galois connection.
Specifically, the relation
f
is (left-)functional iff, for all relations
R
and
S
(of appropriate type),

(31)

f{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptS\hskip 11.99951pt{\equiv}\hskip 11.99951ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS~~.

It is a simple exercise to show that (
31
) is equivalent to the property
f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptI
. (Although
(
31
) doesn’t immediately fit the standard definition of a Galois connection, it can be turned
into standard form by restricting the range of the dummy
R
to relations that satisfy
f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR
, i.e. relations
R
such that
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.)

The converse-dual of (
31
) is also used frequently:
g
is functional iff, for all
R
and
S
,

(32)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptS\hskip 11.99951pt{\equiv}\hskip 11.99951ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}g~~.

Comparing the Galois connections defining the over and under operators
with the Galois connection defining functionality (see (
31
)) suggests a formal relationship
between “division” by a functional relation and composition with the relation’s converse. The precise
form of this relationship is given by the following lemma.

Lemma 33

For all
R
and all functional relations
f
,

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Proof
We use the anti-symmetry of the subset relation. First,

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R

=

\{

domains
~~~\}

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\setminus}R

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR

\Leftarrow

\{

definition and monotonicity
~~~\}

f
is functional .

Second,

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\Leftarrow

\{

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
; monotonicity and transitivity
~~~\}

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\setminus}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR

=

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

\Box

Two lemmas that will be needed later now follow. Lemma
34

allows the converse of a functional relation (i.e. an injective relation) to be cancelled, whilst lemma

35
expresses a distributivity property.

Lemma 34

For all
R
and all functional relations
f
,

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Proof

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)

=

\{

assumption:
f
is functional
~~~\}

f\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)

\subseteq

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

assumption:
f
is functional
~~~\}

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Also,

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

The lemma follows by anti-symmetry of the subset relation.

\Box

Lemma 35

For all
R
and
S
and all functional relations
f
,

R{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf~~.

Proof

R{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf

\Leftarrow

\{

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
, monotonicity
~~~\}

R{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}S

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptS

\Leftarrow

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptS

=

\{

assumption:
f
is functional
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

Also,

R{\setminus}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity,
f\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
~~~\}

R{\setminus}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}(S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}f)

=

\{

factors and cancellation
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

The lemma follows by anti-symmetry of the subset relation.

\Box

The following lemma is crucial to fully understanding Riguet’s “analogie frappante”.
(The lemma is complicated by the fact that it
has five free variables. Simpler, possibly better known, instances can be obtained by instantiating one or
more of
f
,
g
,
U
and
W
to the identity relation.)

Lemma 36

Suppose
f
and
g
are functional. Then, for all
U
,
V
and
W
,

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptU){\setminus}V{/}(W\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

=

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptU\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf){\setminus}(g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptV\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf){/}(g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptW\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Proof
Guided by the assumed functionality of
f
and
g
, we use the rule of indirect equality. Specifically,
we have, for all
R
,
U
,
V
and
W
,

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptU){\setminus}V{/}(W\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

=

\{

assumption:
f
and
g
are functional, (
31
) and (
32
)
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976pt(g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptU){\setminus}V{/}(W\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}})

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptU\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptW\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptV

The lemma follows by instantiating
R
to the left and right sides of the claimed equation, simplifying using
domain calculus, and then applying the
reflexivity and anti-symmetry of the subset relation.

\Box

The final lemma in this section anticipates the discussion of per domains in section
3.8
.

Lemma 37

Suppose relations

,

and

are such that

It follows that

(39)

g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg){\setminus}(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg~~.

Proof
The proof of (

) is as follows.

g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg){\setminus}(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS)

=

\{

factors:
~~~\}

g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\setminus}((f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR){\setminus}(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS))

=

\{

33
with

~~~\}

with
