3.5
Difunctions

Formally, relation
R
is
difunctional
iff

(40)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

As for pers, there are several equivalent definitions of “difunctional”, as formulated below.

Theorem 41

For all
R
, the following statements are all equivalent.

(i)

R
is difunctional (i.e.
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR
) ,

(ii)

R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(iii)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(iv)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,

(v)

R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,

(vi)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,

(vii)

R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.

\Box

3.6
Provisional Orderings

There are various well-known notions of ordering: preorder, partial and linear (aka total) ordering. For
our purposes all of these are too strict — the fact is that, in practice, relations are rarely “total” (for example, not
everyone has a sibling). So, in this section, we introduce the notion of a “provisional
ordering”. The adjective “provisional” has been chosen because the notion “provides” just what we need.
For later use, we state a number of properties but without proof. All proofs can be found in the
companion working document
[
BV22
]
.

The standard definition of an ordering is an anti-symmetric preorder whereby a preorder is required to
be reflexive and transitive. It is the reflexivity requirement that is too strict for our purposes.
So, with the intention of weakening the standard definition of a preorder to requiring reflexivity of a
relation over some superset of its left and right domains, we propose the following definition.

Definition 42

Suppose
T
is a homogeneous relation. Then
T
is said to be a

provisional preorder
if

T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptT\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptT\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptT~~.

\Box

Fig.
2
depicts a provisional preorder on a set of eight elements as a directed graph.
The blue squares should be ignored for the moment. (See the discussion following lemma

48
.) Note that the relation depicted is not a preorder
because it is not reflexive: the top-right node depicts an element that is not in the left or right domain of
the relation.

Figure 2:
A Provisional Preorder

An immediate consequence of the definition is:

Lemma 43

If
T
is a provisional preorder then
T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.

\Box

A trivial property that is nevertheless used frequently:

Lemma 44

T
is a provisional preorder equivales
T^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

is a provisional preorder.

\Box

A preorder is a provisional preorder with left (equally right) domain equal to the identity relation. In
other words, a preorder is a total provisional preorder. It is easy to show that, for any relation
R
, the
relations
R{\setminus}R
and
R{/}R
are preorders. It is also easy to show that
R
is a preorder if and only if
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R

(or equivalently if and only if
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{/}R
). These properties generalise to provisional preorders.

Lemma 45

For all relations
R
, the relations
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R
and
R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
are provisional
preorders.

\Box

Lemma 46

T
is a provisional preorder equivales

T\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\setminus}T\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{/}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\setminus}T{/}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

\Box

Lemma
46
is sometimes used in a form where the domains are replaced by per
domains.

Lemma 47

Suppose
T
is a provisional preorder. Then

T\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\setminus}T\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{/}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\setminus}T{/}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

\Box

Lemma 48

Suppose
T
is a provisional preorder. Then

T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

Hence
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is a per.

\Box

Referring back to fig.
2
, the blue squares depict the equivalence classes of the
symmetric closure of a provisional preorder. As remarked earlier, the depicted relation is not a
preorder; correspondingly, the blue squares depict a truly
partial
equivalence relation.

We assume the reader is familiar with the notions of an ordering and a linear (or total)
ordering. We now extend these notions to provisional orderings. (The at-most relation on the
integers is both anti-symmetric and linear. The at-most relation restricted to some arbitrary
subset of the integers is an example of a linear provisional ordering according to the definition below.)

Definition 49

Suppose
T
is a homogeneous relation of type

A{\sim}A
, for some
A
. Then
T
is said to be
provisionally anti-symmetric
if

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptI_{A}~~.

Also,
T
is said to be a
provisional ordering
if
T
is provisionally anti-symmetric and

T
is a provisional preorder. Finally,
T
is said to be a
linear provisional ordering
if
T
is a
provisional ordering and

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

\Box

Definition
49
weakens the equality in the standard notion of anti-symmetry to an inclusion. The
standard definition of a partial ordering —an anti-symmetric preorder— is weakened accordingly (as
mentioned earlier, in order to “provide” for our needs).

The following lemma anticipates the use of provisional preorders/orderings in examples presented later.

Lemma 50

Suppose
T
is a provisional ordering. Then

T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

\Box

3.7
Squares and Rectangles

We now introduce the notions of a “rectangle” and a “square”; rectangles are typically heterogeneous
whilst squares are, by definition, homogeneous relations. Squares are rectangles; properties of squares
are typically obtained by specialising properties of rectangles.

Definition 51
(Rectangle and Square)

A relation
R
is a
rectangle
iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
. A relation
R

is a
square
iff
R
is a symmetric rectangle.

\Box

It is easily shown that a rectangle is a difunction and a square is a per.

3.8
Isomorphic Relations

The (yet-to-be-defined) cores and indexes of a given relation are not unique; in common mathematical
jargon, they are unique “up to isomorphism”. In order to make this precise, we need to define the
notion of isomorphic relation and establish a number of properties.

Definition 52

Suppose
R
and
S
are two relations (not necessarily of the same type). Then we say
that
R
and
S
are
isomorphic
and write
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cong}\hskip 1.49994ptS
iff

\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt\phi{,}\psi

:

\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

:

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\phi\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\psi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\rangle
~~.

\Box

4
Indexes and Core Relations

This section introduces the notions of “index” and “core” of a relation and records some of their
properties. An “index” is a special case of a “core” of a relation but, in general, it is more useful. For a
detailed account of their properties (including proofs) see
[
BV22
,
BV23
]
.

4.1
Definitions

The definition of an “index” of a relation is as follows.

Definition 53
(Index)

An
index
of a relation
R
is a relation
J
that has the following properties:

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(b)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~,

(c)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(d)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

\Box

Indexes are a special case of what we call “core” relations.

Definition 54
(Core)

Suppose
R
is an arbitrary relation and suppose
C
is a relation
such that

C\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\lambda\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

for some relations
\lambda
and
\rho
satisfying

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\lambda\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957pt\lambda{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\lambda\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957pt\rho{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\rho\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Then
C
is said to be a
core of

R

as witnessed by

\lambda

and

\rho
.

\Box

Note particularly requirement
53
(a). A consequence of this requirement is that an index of a
relation has the same type as the relation, which is not necessarily the case for cores.

An index
J
of a relation
R
is a core of the relation as witnessed by
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.

The property that is common to cores and indexes is captured by the following definition.

Definition 55
(Core Relation)

A relation
R
is a
core relation
iff
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.

\Box

We exploit the fact that both indexes and cores satisfy definition
55
later. (The proof of this fact in

[
BV22
]
assumes that
R
has an index. The —much less straightforward— proof without this
assumption is given in
[
Bac21
]
.)

A number of properties of cores and indexes are needed below. Suppose
C
is a core of
R
as witnessed by

\lambda
and
\rho
. Then

(56)

R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptC\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho~~.

(57)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\lambda{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptC{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\lambda{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\rho{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptC{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\rho{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Suppose
J
is an index of
R
. Then

(58)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

4.2
Indexes of Pers

A relation
R
is a per iff
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
. Using this property, the definition of index can be simplified for pers.

Definition 59
(Index of a Per)

Suppose
P
is a per. Then a (
coreflexive
)

index
of
P
is a relation
J
such that

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptP{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(b)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptJ~~,

(c)

P{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptP~~.

\Box

To our axiom system we add the postulate that every per has a coreflexive index.
We call this the
axiom of choice
.

Axiom 60
(Axiom of Choice)

Every per has a coreflexive index.

\Box

Assuming our axiom of choice, it follows that every relation has an index. Specficially, we have:

Theorem 61

Suppose
J
and
K
are (coreflexive) indices of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
, respectively. Then
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K
is an index of
R
.

\Box

It is also the case that Freyd and Ščedrov’s
[
Fv90
]
so-called “splittings” of pers always exist.

Theorem 62

If per
P
has a coreflexive index
J
, then

P\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951ptJ\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}P)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Thus, assuming the axiom of choice, for all relations
R
,

\mathsf{per}{.}R\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997pt{\left\langle\exists{}f\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\right\rangle}~~.

\Box

The property that
R
is a difunction is equivalent to
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
(and symmetrically to
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
).
Also, since
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
, the
definition of an index of a difunction can be restated as follows.

Definition 63
(Difunction Index)

An index of a difunction
R
is a relation
J
that has the following properties:

(a)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~,

(b)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

(c)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(d)

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

\Box

In the same way that pers are characterised by a single function
f
—see theorem
62
—
difunctions are characterised by a pair of functions
f
and
g
:

Theorem 64

Assuming the axiom of choice (axiom
60
), for all relations
R
,

\mathsf{difunction}{.}R\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997pt{\left\langle\exists\hskip 1.49994pt{}f{,}g\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\right\rangle}~~.

\Box

5
The Diagonal

This section anticipates the study of block-ordered relations
We
introduce the notion of the “diagonal” of a relation in section
5.1
and formulate
some basic properties in section
5.2
.

5.1
Definition and Examples

Straightforwardly from the definition of factors, properties of converse and set intersection,

(65)

R\mbox{ is difunctional\hskip 11.99951pt}{\equiv}\hskip 11.99951ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

More generally, we have:

Lemma 66

For all
R
,
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is difunctional.

Proof
Let
S
denote
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. We have to prove that
S
is difunctional. That is, by definition,

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptS~~.

Since the right side is an intersection, this is equivalent to

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

The first is (almost) trivial:

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS

\subseteq

\{

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
,
S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,

converse, monotonicity
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\subseteq

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

R~~.

In the above calculation, the trick was to replace the outer occurrences of
S
on the left side by
R
and the
middle occurrence by
(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. The replacement is done the opposite way around in the second
calculation.

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
,
S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
, monotonicity and transitivity
~~~\}

(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

converse
~~~\}

R{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

Galois connection
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

cancellation, monotonicity and transitivity
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

\Box

We call the relation
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
the

diagonal
of
R
; Riguet
[
Rig51
]
calls it the “différence”. (Riguet’s definition does
not use factors but is equivalent.; indeed, rewriting the definition using
(
11
) the diagonal of
R
is the “différence” of
R
and
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.)

Definition 67
(Diagonal)

The
diagonal
of relation
R
is the relation
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
.
For brevity,
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
will be denoted by
\Delta{}R
.

\Box

Many readers will be familiar with the decomposition of a preorder into a partial ordering on a set of
equivalence classes. The diagonal of a preorder
T
is the equivalence relation
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. More
generally:

Example 68

The diagonal of a provisional preorder
T
is
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
. This is because,
for an arbitrary relation
T
,

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(T{\setminus}T{/}T)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{=}\hskip 10.49957ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T{\setminus}T{/}T)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

But, if
T
is a provisional preorder,

T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T{\setminus}T{/}T)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

(See lemmas
43
and
46
.)

\Box

Example 69

A particular instance of example
68
is if
G
is the edge
relation of a finite graph. Then
\Delta(G^{*})
is
G^{*}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(G^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{*}
, the relation that holds between nodes
a
and
b
if there
is a path from
a
to
b
and a path from
b
to
a
in the graph. Thus
\Delta(G^{*})
is the equivalence relation that
holds between nodes that are in the same strongly connected component of
G.

\Box

Example 70

In this example, we consider three versions of the less-than relation: the
homogeneous less-than relation on integers, which we denote by
{<}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
, the homogeneous less-than
relation on real numbers, which we denote by
{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
, and the heterogeneous less-than relation on integers
and real numbers, which we denote by
{}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
. Specifically, the relation
{}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
relates integer
m
to real
number
x
when
m\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994ptx
(using the conventional over-loaded notation). We also subscript the at-most
symbol
{\leq}
in the same way in order to indicate the type of the relation in question.

The diagonal of the less-than relation on integers is the predecessor
relation (i.e. it relates integer
m
to integer
n
exactly when
n\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptm{+}1
).
This is because
{<}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}{\setminus}{<}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
, and
{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}{/}{<}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
relates integer
m
to integer
n
exactly when
m\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}\hskip 1.49994ptn{+}1
(where
the subscript
{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}
indicates the type of the ordering). The diagonal is thus functional and injective.

The diagonal of the less-than relation on real numbers is the empty relation. This is because

{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}{\setminus}{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
,
{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}{/}{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
and
{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt{\geq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
. (Again, the subscript indicates the type of the ordering.)

The diagonal of the heterogeneous less-than relation
{}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
relates integer
m
to real number
x
when

m\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994ptx\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm{+}1
. This is equivalent to
\lceil{}x\rceil\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptm{+}1
. The diagonal is thus a difunctional relation characterised
by —in the sense of theorem
64
— the ceiling function
{\left\langle{}x\hskip 1.49994pt{:}{:}\hskip 1.49994pt\lceil{}x\rceil\right\rangle}
and the successor function

{\left\langle{}m\hskip 2.99988pt{:}{:}\hskip 2.99988ptm{+}1\right\rangle}
.

\Box

The following example introduces a general mechanism for constructing illustrative examples of the
concepts introduced later. The example exploits the observation that
\Delta{}R
is injective if the preorder
R{\setminus}R

is anti-symmetric; that is,
\Delta{}R
is injective if
R{\setminus}R
is a partial ordering. (Equivalently,
\Delta{}R
is functional if

R{/}R
is a partial ordering.) We leave the straightforward proof to the reader.

Example 71

Suppose
\mathcal{X}
is a finite type. We use dummy
x
to range over
elements of type
\mathcal{X}
. Let
\mathcal{S}
denote a subset of
2^{\mathcal{X}}
.
Let
\mathsf{in}
denote the membership relation of type
\mathcal{X}{\sim}\mathcal{S}
. That is, if
S
is a subset of
\mathcal{S}
,
x{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}
exactly
when
x
is an element of the set
S
. The relation
\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
is the subset relation of type
\mathcal{S}{\sim}\mathcal{S}
.

(Conventionally,
\mathsf{in}
is denoted by the
symbol “
{\in}
” and one writes
x{\in}S
instead of
x{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}
. Also, the relation
\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
is conventionally denoted by
the symbol “
{\subseteq}
”. That is, if
S
and
S^{\prime}
are both elements of
\mathcal{S}
,
S{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
exactly when
S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\prime}
.
Were we to adopt conventional practice, the
overloading of the notation that occurs is likely to cause confusion, so we choose to avoid it.)

The relation
\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
is anti-symmetric. As a consequence,
\Delta\mathsf{in}
is injective. (Equivalently,
(\Delta\mathsf{in})^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is
functional.) Specifically, for all
x
of type
\mathcal{X}
and
S
of type
\mathcal{S}
,

x{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta\mathsf{in}\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963ptx{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982pt{\left\langle\forall{}S^{\prime}\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptx{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988ptS{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}\right\rangle}~~,

where dummy
S^{\prime}
ranges over elements of
\mathcal{S}
. Using conventional notation, the right side of this equation is
recognised as the definition of a minimum, and one might write

x\hskip 2.99988pt[\![\Delta\mathsf{in}]\!]\hskip 2.99988pt{}S\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957ptS\hskip 2.99988pt{\begin{picture}(0.0,0.0)\put(0.0,0.7){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(0.0,0.3){\line{1}{0}{1.0}}\put(1.0,0.7){\line{0}{1}{0.2}}\put(1.0,0.3){\line{0}{-1}{0.2}}\end{picture}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\left\langle\mathsf{MIN}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994ptx{\in}S^{\prime}\hskip 1.49994pt{:}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\prime}\right\rangle}

where the venturi tube “
” indicates an equality assuming the well-definedness of the expression on its
right side.

Figure 3:
Diagonal of an Instance of the Membership Relation

Fig.
3
shows a particular instance. The set
\mathcal{X}
is the set of numbers from
0
to
3
. The
set
\mathcal{S}
is a subset of
2^{\{0{,}1{,}2{,}3\}}
; the chosen subset and the relation
\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
for this choice are depicted by the
directed graph forming the central portion of fig.
3
. The relation
\Delta\mathsf{in}
of type
\mathcal{X}\hskip 1.49994pt{\sim}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{S}
is
depicted by the undirected graph whereby the atoms of the relation are depicted as rectangles. Note
that the numbers
0
and
3
are not related by
\Delta\mathsf{in}
to any of the elements of
\mathcal{S}
.

\Box

5.2
Basic Properties

Primarily for notational convenience, we note a simple property of the diagonal:

Lemma 72

(\Delta{}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\Delta(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

Proof

(\Delta{}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

definition and distributivity
~~~\}

R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

definition
~~~\}

\Delta(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

\Box

A consequence of lemma
72
is that we can write
\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
without ambiguity. This we do from now
on.

Very straightforwardly, the relation
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is a per if
R
is difunctional. For a difunctional relation
R
,
the relation
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is the left per domain of
R
. (Symmetrically,
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR
is the right per
domain of
R
. See theorem
41
, parts (iv) and (vi).)
Thus
\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is the left per domain of the diagonal of
R
.
The following lemma is the basis of the construction, in certain cases, of an economic representation
of the diagonal of
R
and, hence, of
R
itself.

Lemma 73

For all relations
R
,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Dually,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

Proof
We prove the first equation by mutual inclusion. First,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R
is difunctional, theorem
41
; definition: (
23
)
~~~\}

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R

\Leftarrow

\{

domains and monotonicity
~~~\}

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R

=

\{

definition of
R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R
, converse and factors
~~~\}

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
;
\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R
and cancellation
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

Second,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R
is difunctional, theorem
41
~~~\}

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

domains and definition: (
23
)
~~~\}

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity and converse
~~~\}

R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R

=

\{

definition of diagonal
~~~\}

R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
; converse
~~~\}

R/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

cancellation; factors
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR/{\hskip-4.2679pt}/{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

\Leftarrow

\{

cancellation and
\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

The remaining three equalities are simple consequences of the properties of converse, pers and
coreflexives.

\Box

The following corollary of lemma
73
proves to be crucial later:

Lemma 74

For all relations
R
,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Dually,

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Proof
The proof is by mutual implication:

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

lemma
73
and Leibniz
~~~\}

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}

=

\{

dual of (
30
)
~~~\}

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

Leibniz
~~~\}

((\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

dual of (
30
) with
R\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R
and
R\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR
~~~\}

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

\Box

5.3
Reduction to the Core

In this section our goal is to prove that if
J
is an index of relation
R
then
\Delta{}J
is an index of
\Delta{}R
.
Instantiating definition
63
with
J{,}R\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J{,}\Delta{}R
the properties we have to prove are as follows.

(a)

\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R~~,

(b)

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R~~.

(c)

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~,

(d)

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Of these, the hardest to prove is (b). For properties (a), (c) and (d), all we need is that
J
is an arbitrary
index of
R
. For property (b), we use the fact that
an index of an arbitrary relation
R
is defined to be
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}K
where
J
is an index of
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
and
K
is an index of

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}
.

We begin with the easier properties.

Lemma 75

Suppose
J
is an index of
R
. Then

\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R~~.

Proof

\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R

=

\{

definition
67
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\setminus}J{/}J)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

domains
~~~\}

J\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\setminus}J{/}J)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\subseteq}\hskip 7.4997ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

J
is an index of
R
, so
J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
; monotonicity
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(J{\setminus}J{/}J)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

converse
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR

=

\{

J
is an index of
R
, definition
53
(b); per domains
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR~~.

Continuing with the left side of the inclusion:

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

=

\{

domains
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

=

J
is an index of

; definition

(c) and (d)

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\setminus}J{/}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

domains and cancellation

Lemma 76

Suppose
J
is an index of
R
. Then

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Dually,

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Proof

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R
is a difunction, theorem
41
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

lemma
73
(and symmetry)
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

by lemma
75
and monotonicity,
(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
(since
\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ
)
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

J
is an index of
R
, definition
53
(c)
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
(since
\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ
)
~~~\}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

\Box

In order to prove (b), we prove a more general theorem on cores. First, a lemma:

Lemma 77

Suppose

,

,

Proof

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

(
30
)

(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

,

, and domains

\{

(
56
)
~~~\}

\rho{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptC\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho){\setminus}(\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptC\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho){/}(\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptC\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho)\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\lambda{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

lemma
36
with

\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptC{\setminus}C{/}C\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\lambda~~.

Theorem 78

Suppose
R
,
C
,
\lambda
and
\rho
are as in definition
54
. Then

\Delta{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\lambda^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}C\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho\hskip 11.99951pt{\wedge}\hskip 11.99951pt\Delta{}C\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\lambda\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

In words, if
\lambda
and
\rho
witness that
C
is a core of
R
, then
\lambda

Theorem 81

Suppose
J
is an index of
R
. Then

\Delta{}J\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 13.49945pt{\wedge}\hskip 13.49945pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

Proof
We first prove, by mutual implication, that the two equations are equivalent. Assume that

\Delta{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}~~.

Then,

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

assumption
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ
, so
(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

(\Delta{}J){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
; domains
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

\{

J
is an index of

, definition
53
(c) and (d)
~~~\}

\Delta{}J~~.

Conversely, assume

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}

\subseteq

lemma
75
and monotonicity

, domains
