\Box

6
Block-Ordered Relations

In general, dividing a subset of a set
A
into blocks
is formulated by specifying a functional relation
f
, say, with source
4
4

4

In
the terminology we use, a
relation of type
A{\sim}B
has
target

A
and
source

B
.
the set
A
;
elements
a0
and
a1
are in the same block equivales
f{.}a0
and
f{.}a1
are both defined and
f{.}a0\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptf{.}a1
.
In mathematical terminology, a functional relation
f
defines the
partial equivalence relation

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
and
the “blocks” are the equivalence classes of
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
. (Partiality means that some elements may not be in an
equivalence class.)

Given functional relations
f
and
g
with sources
A
and
B
, respectively, and equal left domains, relation

R
of type
A{\sim}B
is said to be
block-structured
by
f
and
g
if there is a relation
S
such that
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
.
Informally, whether or not
a
and
b
are related by
R
depends entirely on the “block”
(f{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt,\hskip 1.49994ptg{.}b)
to which
they belong. Note that it is not required that
f
and
g
be total functions: it suffices that
f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
g{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.
The type of
S
is
C{\sim}C
where
C
includes
\{a{:}\hskip 4.49982pta\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pta{:}\hskip 4.49982ptf{.}a\}
(equally
\{b{:}\hskip 4.49982ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptb{:}\hskip 4.49982ptg{.}b\}
).

Definition 82
(Block-Ordered Relation)

Suppose
T
is a relation of type
C{\sim}C
,
f
is a relation of type
C{\sim}A
and
g
is a relation
of type
C{\sim}B
. Suppose further that
T
is a provisional ordering and
that
f
and
g
are functional and onto the domain of
T
. That is, suppose

(83)

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Then we say that the relation
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
is a
block-ordered relation
. A relation
R
of
type
A{\sim}B
is said to be
block-ordered
by
f
,
g
and
T
if
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
and

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
is a block-ordered relation.

\Box

The archetypical example of a block-ordered relation is
a preorder. Informally, if
R
is a preorder, its symmetric closure
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is an equivalence relation,
and the relation
R
defines a partial ordering on the equivalence classes. Equivalently, if a representative element
is chosen for each equivalence class, the relation
R
is a partial ordering on the representatives.
Theorem
84
makes this precise.

Theorem 84

Suppose
T
is a provisional preorder and suppose
J
is a (coreflexive) index of

T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
. Then
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J
is an index of
T
and is a provisional ordering. Hence,
T
is a
block-ordered relation.

Proof
That
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J
is an index of
T
is the combination of lemma
48
and
theorem
61
. It is a provisional preorder because
T
is a preorder and
J
is coreflexive.
So, it remains to show that
J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J
is provisionally anti-symmetric. That is, we must show that

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptI
.

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

J
is coreflexive, distributivity
~~~\}

J{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}J

\subseteq

\{

48
~~~\}

J\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptJ

=

\{

J
is an index of
T{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
, definition
59
(b) with
P\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}
~~~\}

J

\subseteq

\{

J
is coreflexive
~~~\}

I~~.

\Box

Identifying a block-ordering of a relation —if it exists— is important for efficiency. Although a
relation is defined to be a set of pairs, relations —even relations on finite sets— are rarely stored as such;
instead some base set of pairs is stored and an algorithm used to generate, on demand, additional
information about the relation. This is particularly so of ordering relations. For example, a test
m\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994ptn
on
integers
m
and
n
in a computer program is never implemented as a table lookup; instead an algorithm is
used to infer from the basic relations
0\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994pt1
together with the internal representation of
m
and
n
what the
value of the test is. In the case of block-structured relations, functional relations
f
and
g
define partial
equivalence relations
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
and
g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
on their respective sources. (The relations
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
and
g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
are
partial because
f
and
g
are not required to be total.) Combining the functional relations with an
ordering relation on their (common) target is an effective way of implementing a relation
(assuming the ordering relation is also implemented effectively).

Example 85

Suppose
G
is the edge relation of a finite graph. The
relation
G^{*}
is, of course, a preorder and so is block-ordered. The block-ordering of
G^{*}
given by theorem

84
is, however, not very useful. For practical
purposes a block-ordering constructed from
G
(rather than
G^{*}
) is preferable. Here we outline how this is
done.

Recall from example
69
, that the diagonal
\Delta(G^{*})
is the relation
G^{*}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(G^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{*}

and that this is an equivalence relation on the nodes of
G
, whereby the equivalence classes are
the
strongly connected components
of
G
. Let
N
denote the nodes of
G
and
C
denote the set of strongly
connected components of
G.
By theorem
62
, there is a function
\mathsf{sc}
of type
C{\leftarrow}N
such that

(86)

G^{*}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(G^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{*}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\mathsf{sc}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{sc}~~.

The relation
\mathcal{A}
defined by

\mathsf{sc}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptG\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{sc}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\cap}\hskip 5.99976pt{\neg}I_{C}

is a homogeneous relation on the strongly connected components of
G
, i.e. a relation of type
C{\sim}C
.
Informally, it is a graph obtained from the graph
G
by coalescing the nodes in a strongly connected
component of
G
into a single node whilst retaining the edges of
G
that connect nodes in distinct strongly
connected components. A fundamental theorem is that

(87)

G^{*}\hskip 8.99963pt{=}\hskip 8.99963pt\mathsf{sc}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{A}^{*}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathsf{sc}~~.

Moreover,
\mathcal{A}
is acyclic. That is,

(88)

I_{C}\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt\mathcal{A}^{+}\hskip 7.4997pt{=}\hskip 7.4997pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

(See
[
BDGv22
,
Bac22
]
for the details of the proof of (
87
) and (
88
). In fact
the theorem is valid for all relations
G
; finiteness is not required.)

The relation
\mathcal{A}^{*}
is, of course, transitive. It is also reflexive; combined with its acyclicity, it follows that

(89)

\mathcal{A}^{*}\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994pt(\mathcal{A}^{*})^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptI_{C}~~.

That is,
\mathcal{A}^{*}
is a (total) provisional ordering on
C.
The conclusion is that
G^{*}
is
block-ordered by
\mathsf{sc}
,
\mathsf{sc}
and
\mathcal{A}^{*}
.

Informally, a finite graph can always be decomposed into its strongly connected components together
with an acyclic graph connecting the components.

Although the informal interpretation of this theorem is well-known, the formal proof is non-trivial.
Although not formulated in the same way, it is essentially the “transitive reduction” of an arbitrary (not
necessarily acyclic) graph formulated by Aho, Garey and Ullman
[
AGU72
, Theorem 2]
.

The decomposition (
87
) is (implicitly) exploited when computing
the inverse
\mathbf{A}^{{-}1}
of a real matrix
\mathbf{A}
in order to minimise storage requirements: using an elimination
technique, a so-called “product form” is computed for each strongly connected component, whilst the
process of “forward substitution” is applied to the acyclic-graph structure.

\Box

Theorem
90
makes precise the statement that block orderings —where they exist—
are unique “up to isomorphism”.

\phi^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\phi\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\phi

=

\{

definition of
\phi
~~~\}

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumptions:
f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

T\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumption:
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptk
, (
93
) and converse
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptk\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptk^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

assumption:
h\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptk\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptk^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pth^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptS

=

\{

definition of
\phi
~~~\}

\phi{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}S~~.

\Box

6.1
Pair Algebras and Galois Connections

In order to find lots of examples of block-ordered relations one need look no further than the theory of
Galois connections (which are, of course, ubiquitous). In this section, we briefly review the notion of a
“pair algebra” —due to Hartmanis and Stearns
[
HS64
,
HS66
]
— and its relation to Galois
connections.

Hartmanis and Stearns limited their analysis to finite, complete posets, and their
analysis was less general than is possible. This work was extended in
[
Bac98
]
to non-finite posets and
the current section is a short extract.

A Galois connection involves two posets
(\mathcal{A},\sqsubseteq)
and (
\mathcal{B}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}
) and
two functions,
F\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{A}{\leftarrow}\mathcal{B}
and
G\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{B}{\leftarrow}\mathcal{A}
. These four components together form a

Galois connection
iff for all
b{\in}\mathcal{B}
and
a{\in}\mathcal{A}

(97)

F{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 2.99988pt{\equiv}\hskip 2.99988ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}a~~.

We refer to
F
as the
lower adjoint
and to
G
as the
upper adjoint
.

A Galois connection is thus a connection between two functions between posets.
Typical accounts of the properties of Galois connections (e.g.
[
GHK+80
]
) focus
on the properties of these
functions
. For example, given a function
F
, one may ask
whether
F
is a lower adjoint in a Galois connection. The question posed
by Hartmanis and Stearns was, however, rather different.

To motivate their question, note that the statement
F{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta
defines a

relation
between
\mathcal{B}
and
\mathcal{A}
. So too does
b\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}a
. The existence of a Galois
connection states that these two relations are equal. A natural question is therefore:
under which conditions does an arbitrary (binary) relation between two posets
define a Galois connection between the sets?

Exploring the question in more detail leads to two separate questions.
The first is: suppose
R
is a relation between posets

(\mathcal{A},\sqsubseteq)
and (
\mathcal{B}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}
). What is a necessary and sufficient condition that there exist
a function
F
such that

(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptF{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\mbox{~~~~?}

The second is the dual of the first: given relation
R
,
what is a necessary and sufficient condition that there exist a function
G
such
that

(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}a\mbox{~~~~?}

The conjunction of these two conditions is a necessary and sufficient condition for a
relation
R
to define a Galois connection. Such a relation is called a
pair algebra
.

Example 98

It is easy to demonstrate that the two questions are separate. To this end,
fig.
4
depicts two posets and a relation between them. The posets are
\{\alpha{,}\beta\}
and
\{\mathsf{A}{,}\mathsf{B}\}
; both are
ordered lexicographically: the reflexive-transitive reduction of the lexicographic ordering is depicted by
the directed edges. The relation of type
\{\alpha{,}\beta\}{\sim}\{\mathsf{A}{,}\mathsf{B}\}
is depicted by the undirected edges.

Figure 4:
A Relation on Two Posets

Let the relation be denoted by
R
. Define the function
F
of type
\{\alpha{,}\beta\}\hskip 1.49994pt{\leftarrow}\hskip 1.49994pt\{\mathsf{A}{,}\mathsf{B}\}
by
F{.}\mathsf{B}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\alpha
and
F{.}\mathsf{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\beta
. Then it
is easy to check that. for
a{\in}\{\alpha{,}\beta\}
and
b{\in}\{\mathsf{A}{,}\mathsf{B}\}
,

(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptF{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta~~.

(There are just four cases to be considered.) On the other hand, there is no function
G
of type

\{\mathsf{A}{,}\mathsf{B}\}\hskip 1.49994pt{\leftarrow}\hskip 1.49994pt\{\alpha{,}\beta\}
such that

(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}a~~.

To check that this is indeed the case, it suffices to check that the assignment
G{.}\mathsf{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\alpha
is invalid (because
\alpha\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\alpha
but
(\alpha,\mathsf{A})\hskip 1.49994pt{\not\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR
) and
the assignment
G{.}\mathsf{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt\beta
is also invalid (because
\alpha\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\beta
but
(\alpha,\mathsf{A})\hskip 1.49994pt{\not\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR
).

\Box

Example 99

A less artificial, general way to demonstrate that the two questions are
separate is to consider the membership relation. Specifically, suppose
\mathcal{S}
is a set. Then the membership
relation, denoted as usual by the —overloaded— symbol “
{\in}
”, is a heterogeneous relation of type
\mathcal{S}\hskip 1.49994pt{\sim}\hskip 1.49994pt2^{\mathcal{S}}

(where
2^{\mathcal{S}}
denotes the type of subsets of
\mathcal{S}
). Now, for all
x
of type
\mathcal{S}
and
X
of type
2^{\mathcal{S}}
,

x\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptX\hskip 4.49982pt{\equiv}\hskip 4.49982pt\{x\}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptX~~.

The right side of this equation has the form
F{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta
where
F
is the function that maps an element into a
singleton set and the ordering is the subset ordering. Also, its left side has the form
(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR
, where the
relation
R
is the membership relation and
a
and
b
are
x
and
X
, respectively. (This is where the
overloading of notation can become confusing, for which our apologies!) It is, however, not possible to
express
x\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptX
in the form
x\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}X
(except in the trivial cases where
\mathcal{S}
has cardinality at most one). We
leave the proof to the reader.

\Box

Example 100

An example of a Galois connection is the definition
of the ceiling function on real numbers: for all real numbers
x
,
\lceil{}x\rceil
is an integer such that, for all
integers
m
,

x\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976pt\lceil{}x\rceil\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm~~.

To properly fit the definition of a Galois connection, it is necessary to make explicit the implicit coercion
from integers to real numbers in the left side of this equation. Specifically, we have, for all real numbers
x

and integers
m
,

x~{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}~\mathsf{real}{.}m\hskip 11.99951pt{\equiv}\hskip 11.99951pt\lceil{}x\rceil~{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}~m

where
\mathsf{real}
denotes the function that “coerces” an integer to a real, and
\leq_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
and
\leq_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
denote
the (homogeneous) at-most relations on, respectively, real numbers and integers. If, however, we
consider the symbol “
{\leq}
” on the left side of the equation to denote the heterogeneous at-most
relation of type
{\mathsf{I\!R}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\sim}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}
, the fact that

x\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957pt\lceil{}x\rceil~{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}~m

gives a representation of the (heterogeneous) “
{\leq}
” relation of type
{\mathsf{I\!R}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\sim}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}
as a block-ordered
relation: referring to definition
82
, the provisional
ordering is
{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
,
f
is the ceiling function and
g
is the identity function.

More interesting is if we take the contrapositive. We have, for all real numbers
x
and integers
m
,

m\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994ptx\hskip 7.4997pt{\equiv}\hskip 7.4997ptm\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994pt\lceil{}x\rceil{-}1~~.

On the right of this equation is the (homogeneous) at-most relation on integers. On the left is the
(heterogeneous) less-than relation of type
{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\sim}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mathsf{I\!R}}
. The equation demonstrates that this relation is
block-ordered; the “blocks” of real numbers being all the numbers that have the same ceiling. (The
functional
f
is the identity function, the functional
g
maps real number
x
to
\lceil{}x\rceil{-}1
and the provisional
ordering is the ordering
\leq_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
.) The example is interesting because the (homogeneous) less-than relation
on real numbers is
not
block-ordered. This is because its diagonal is empty. See examples

70
and
127
.

\Box

Returning to the discussion immediately preceding example
98
, the two separate questions are each of interest in their own right: a
positive answer to either question may predict that a given relation has a block-ordering of a specific
form: in the case of the first question, where the functional
g
in definition
82
is
the identity function, and, in the case of the second question, where the functional
f
in definition

82
is the identity function. In both cases, a further step is to check the
requirement on
f
and
g
: in the first case, one has to check that the function
F
is surjective and in the
second case that the function
G
is surjective. (A Galois connection is said to be “perfect” if both
F
and
G
are
surjective.) For example, the fact that

x\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm\hskip 11.99951pt{\equiv}\hskip 11.99951ptx~{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}~\mathsf{real}{.}m

does
not
define a block-ordering because the function
\mathsf{real}
is not surjective.

The relevant theory predicting exactly when the first of the two questions has
a positive answer is as follows. Suppose
(\mathcal{B},\sqsubseteq)
is a complete poset.
Let
{{\sqcap}}
denote the infimum operator for
\mathcal{B}
and suppose
p
is a predicate on
\mathcal{B}
. Then we
define
inf-preserving
by

(101)

p\mbox{ is inf-preserving\hskip 8.99963pt}{\equiv}\hskip 8.99963pt{\left\langle\forall{}g\hskip 4.49982pt{:}{:}\hskip 4.49982ptp{.}({\sqcap}g)\hskip 2.99988pt{\equiv}\hskip 2.99988pt{\left\langle\forall{}x\hskip 2.99988pt{:}{:}\hskip 2.99988ptp{.}(g{.}x)\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

So, for a given
a
, the predicate
\langle{}b{:}{:}\hskip 2.99988pt(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\rangle
is inf-preserving equivales

{\left\langle\forall{}g\hskip 7.4997pt{:}{:}\hskip 7.4997pt(a\hskip 1.49994pt,\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqcap}g)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976pt{\left\langle\forall{}x\hskip 2.99988pt{:}{:}\hskip 2.99988pt(a\hskip 1.49994pt,\hskip 1.49994ptg{.}x)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\right\rangle}\right\rangle}~~.

Then we have:

Theorem 102

Suppose
\mathcal{A}
is a set and
(\mathcal{B},\sqsubseteq)
is a complete poset.
Suppose
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt\mathcal{A}{{\times}}\mathcal{B}
is a relation between the two sets.
Define
F
by

(103)

F{.}a\hskip 10.49957pt{=}\hskip 10.49957pt{\left\langle{\sqcap}b\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982pt(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{:}\hskip 4.49982ptb\right\rangle}~~.

Then the following two statements are equivalent.

•

{\left\langle\forall\hskip 1.49994pt{}a{,}b\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pta{\in}\mathcal{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{\wedge}\hskip 1.49994ptb{\in}\mathcal{B}\hskip 5.99976pt{:}\hskip 5.99976pt(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptF{.}a\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptb\right\rangle}
.

•

For all
a
, the predicate
\langle{}b{:}{:}\hskip 2.99988pt(a,b)\hskip 1.49994pt{\in}\hskip 1.49994ptR\rangle
is inf-preserving.

\Box

The answer to the second question is, of course, obtained by formulating the dual of theorem
102
.

In general, for most relations occurring in practical information systems the answer to the pair-algebra
questions will be negative: the required inf- and sup-preserving properties just do not hold.
However, a common way to define a pair algebra is to extend a given
relation to a relation between sets in such a way that the infimum and supremum preserving
properties are automatically satisfied. Hartmanis and Stearns’
[
HS64
,
HS66
]
solution to the state
assignment problem was to consider the lattice of partitions of a given set; in so-called “concept analysis”,
the technique is to extend a given relation to a relation between rectangles.

An important property of Galois connections is the theorem we call the “unity of opposites”:
if
F
and
G
are the adjoint functions in a Galois connection of the posets
(\mathcal{A},\sqsubseteq)
and (
\mathcal{B}{,}{\preceq}
),
then there is an isomorphism between the posets
(F{.}\mathcal{B}\hskip 1.49994pt,\hskip 1.49994pt\sqsubseteq)
and (
G{.}\mathcal{A}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}
). (
F{.}\mathcal{B}
denotes the “image” of the
function
F
, and similarly for
G{.}\mathcal{A}
.) Knowledge of the unity-of-opposites theorem suggests theorem

90
, which expresses an isomorphism between different representations of
block-ordered relations.

6.2
Analogie Frappante

In this section, we relate block-orderings to diagonals. The main result is theorem
114
; we
call theorem
114
the “analogie frappante” because it generalises Riguet’s “analogie
frappante” connecting “relation de Ferrers” to diagonals.

Some elements of the following lemma have been recorded earlier by Winter
[
Win04
]
. We think
the overlap is justified because Winter’s calculations make very heavy use of complementation whereas our
calculations avoid its use altogether.

Lemma 104

Suppose
T
is a provisional ordering of type
C{\sim}C
.
Suppose also that
f
and
g
are functional and onto the domain of
T
.
That is, suppose
6
6

6

The ordering
T
must be homogeneous
but
f
and
g
may be heterogeneous and of different type, so long as both have target
C
.
that

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Let
R
denote
f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
. Then

(105)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

(106)

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\mbox{~, and}

(107)

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\Delta{}R~~,

(108)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~,

(109)

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg~~.

Proof
Property (
105
) is a straightforward application of domain calculus:

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

definition:
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}

=

\{

domains (specifically,
\left[\hskip 2.99988pt(U{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}V){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(U{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptV){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt\right]
and

A crucial step in the above calculation is the use of the property

for all relations
U
and
V
and coreflexive relations
p
and

. This is a frequently used property of domain
restriction.

We now prove the converse of lemma
104
.

Lemma 111

A relation
R
is block-ordered if
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.

Proof
Suppose
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
. Our task is to construct relations
f
,
g
and
T
such that

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg~~,

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptI\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptT\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 10.49957pt{\wedge}\hskip 10.49957ptT{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptT\mbox{~~~and}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Since
\Delta{}R
is difunctional, theorem
64
is the obvious place to start. Applying the theorem,
we can construct
f
and
g
such that

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Using standard properties of the domain operators together with the assumption that

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
, it follows that

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\wedge}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

It remains to construct the provisional ordering
T
. The appropriate construction is suggested by lemma

104
, in particular (
106
). Specifically, we define
T
by the equation

(112)

T\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

The proof that
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
is by mutual inclusion. First note that

(113)

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R

since

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

=

\{

(
112
)
~~~\}

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R~~.

So

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

\subseteq

\{

(
113
) and
\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\subseteq

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

R~~.

Also,

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

=

\{

(
113
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R

=

\{

per domains
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R

=

\{

assumption:
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

, lemma

(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R

\Delta{}R
is difunctional, theorem

(with

\Leftarrow

\{

~~~\}

\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

\Leftarrow

\{

\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R
, monotonicity
~~~\}

R{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR

=

\{

cancellation
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

Combining the two inclusions we conclude that indeed
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
.

We now establish the requirements on
T
. First,

T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

definition and converse
~~~\}

g\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 7.4997pt{\cap}\hskip 7.4997ptf\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\subseteq

\{

modular law
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 5.99976pt{\cap}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\cap}\hskip 5.99976pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\subseteq

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
, monotonicity and cancellation
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(R\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
~~~\}

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Thus
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
. So
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptI
. Now

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

infima and
f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is coreflexive
~~~\}

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptT

\Leftarrow

\{

domains
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptT

\Leftarrow

\{

definition of
T
and monotonicity
~~~\}

f\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

\Leftarrow

\{

f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
, domains and monotonicity
~~~\}

g^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

f^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt\Delta{}R
~~~\}

\Delta{}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
, converse
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

So, by anti-symmetry we have established that
T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
. Since also
f{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
, we conclude from the
definition of
T
and properties of domains that

T\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})~~.

The final task is to show that
T
is transitive:

T{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}T

=

\{

definition
~~~\}

g\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

g\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\subseteq

\{

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR
~~~\}

g\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\subseteq

\{

factors
~~~\}

g\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

definition
~~~\}

T~~.

\Box

It is interesting to reflect on the proof of lemma
111
. The hardest part is to find appropriate
definitions of
f
,
g
and
T
such that
R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
. The key to constructing
f
and
g
is Riguet’s “analogie
frappante”
[
Rig51
]
whereby he introduced the “différence” —the diagonal
\Delta{}R
— of the relation
R
.
Expressing the diagonal in terms of factors as we have done makes many parts of the calculations very
straightforward. One much less straightforward step is the use of lemma
74
in the proof that

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
. Note how calculational needs drive the search for the lemma: in order to simplify the
inclusion it is necessary to expose the term
R{\setminus}R{/}R
on the right side, and that is precisely what the lemma
enables.

We conclude with the theorem that we call the “analogie frappante”. It is not the theorem that Riguet
suggested but we have chosen to give it this name in order to recognise Riguet’s contribution.

Theorem 114
(Analogie Frappante)

A relation
R
is block-ordered if and only
if
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
.

Proof
Lemma
104
establishes “only-if” and lemma
111
establishes “if”.

\Box

Example 115

A generic way to construct examples of relations that are not
block-ordered is to exploit example
71
. In order to avoid unnecessary repetition, we
refer the reader to that example for the definition of the relation
\mathsf{in}
given a finite set
\mathcal{X}
and a set
\mathcal{S}
of
subsets of
\mathcal{X}
.

Recall that the diagonal
\Delta\mathsf{in}
of type
\mathcal{X}{\sim}\mathcal{S}
is injective. It follows that the
size of
(\Delta\mathsf{in}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
is at most the size of
\mathcal{S}
. If, however, the set
\mathcal{S}
has
\mathcal{X}
as one of its elements, the size of
\mathsf{in}{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

equals the size of
\mathcal{X}
. Theorem
114
thus predicts that, if
\mathcal{X}
is an element of
\mathcal{S}
, a necessary
condition for
\mathsf{in}
to be block-ordered is that the sizes of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
must be equal; conversely, if
\mathcal{X}
is an
element of
\mathcal{S}
,
\mathsf{in}
is not block-ordered if the sizes of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
are different.

Fig.
3
(example
71
) shows that, even if the sizes of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
are
equal, the relation
\mathsf{in}
may not be block-ordered: as remarked then, for the choice of
\mathcal{S}
shown in fig.
3
,
\mathsf{in}{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
and
(\Delta\mathsf{in}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}
are different since
0
and
3
are elements of the former but not the
latter.

It is straightforward to construct instances of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
such that the relation
\mathsf{in}
is block-ordered. It
suffices to ensure that three conditions are satisfied:
\mathcal{X}
is an element of
\mathcal{S}
, the sizes of
\mathcal{X}
and
\mathcal{S}
are equal,
and, for each
x
in
\mathcal{X}
, the set of sets represented by
(x{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\mathsf{in}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}
is totally ordered. Fig.
5
is
one such. Referring to definition
82
, the functional
f
is
\Delta\mathsf{in}^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

(depicted by rectangles) and the functional
g
is
I_{\mathcal{S}}
; the ordering relation is
the subset relation
\mathsf{in}{\setminus}\mathsf{in}
(depicted by the directed graph).

Figure 5:
A Block-Ordered Membership Relation

\Box

The following theorem is a corollary of theorem
78
. In combination with theorem

114
it states that a relation is block-ordered iff its core is block-ordered. Testing whether or
not a given relation is block-ordered can thus be decomposed into computing a core of the relation
and then testing whether or not that is block-ordered. (For practical purposes computing an index of the
relation is to be preferred.)

Theorem 116

Suppose
R
is an arbitrary relation and suppose
C
is a core of
R
as
witnessed by
\lambda
and
\rho
. Then

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957ptC{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}C){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}~~.

Dually,

R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}R){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957ptC{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}C){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{>}$}}}~~.

Proof
Suppose
R
,
C
,
\lambda
and
\rho
are as in definition
54
. Then

C{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\Delta{}C){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

=

\{

definition
54
and theorem
78

~~~\}

(\lambda\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt(\lambda\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt\rho^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

Leibniz

follows by mutual implication. The dual follows by instantiating
R
to
