and applying the properties of
converse.

\Box

By combining the definition of block-ordering with theorem
78
, it is immediately clear that

R
is block-ordered if its core
C
is a provisional ordering. In general, a core of a block-ordered relation will not
be a provisional ordering. This is because the types of the targets of the components
\lambda
and
\rho
in the
definition of a core are not required to be the same; on the other hand, orderings are required to be
homogeneous relations. However by carefully restricting the choice of core, it is possible to construct a
core that is indeed a provisional ordering.

Theorem 117

Suppose
R
is an arbitrary relation. Then if
R
is block-ordered it
has a core that is a provisional ordering.

Proof
Suppose
R
is block-ordered. That is, suppose that
f
,
g
and
T
are relations such that
T
is a
provisional ordering,

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg

and

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Then, by lemma
104
,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\prec}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf
and ,
R{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{\succ}$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
. Thus
f
and
g
satisfy the conditions
for witnessing a core
C
of
R
. (Cf. definition
54
with
\lambda{,}\rho\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptf{,}g
.) Consequently,

C

=

\{

definition
54
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

R\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg
~~~\}

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

f\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptf^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptf{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg{\mbox{\raisebox{1.70717pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle{<}$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptg\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptg^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
~~~\}

(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptT\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt(T\hskip 1.49994pt{\cap}\hskip 1.49994ptT^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})

=

\{

T
is a provisional ordering, lemma
50
and domains
~~~\}

T~~.

We conclude that
C
is the provisional ordering
T
.

\Box

Combining theorem
117
with the theorem that all cores of a given relation are
isomorphic, we conclude that any core of a
block-ordered relation is isomorphic to a provisional ordering. Loosely speaking, block-ordered
relations are provisional orderings up to isomorphism and reduction to the core.

Example 118

From the Galois connection, for all reals
x
and integers
m
,

\lceil{}x\rceil\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptx\hskip 1.49994pt{\leq}\hskip 1.49994ptm

defining the ceiling function, we deduce that the heterogeneous relation
{}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
has core the provisional
ordering
{\leq}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
. This is because the ceiling function is surjective. Its core in
not
the ordering
{\leq}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
because
the coercion
\mathsf{real}
from integers to reals is not surjective. (See also example
100
.)

On the other hand, if a Galois connection

F{.}b\hskip 1.49994pt{\sqsubseteq}\hskip 1.49994pta\hskip 2.99988pt{\equiv}\hskip 2.99988ptb\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}\hskip 1.49994ptG{.}a

of posets
(\mathcal{A},\sqsubseteq)
and (
\mathcal{B}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994pt{\preceq}
) is “perfect” (i.e. both
F
and
G
are surjective), both the orderings
{\sqsubseteq}
and
{\preceq}
are
cores of the defined heterogeneous relation. That the orderings are isomorphic is an instance of the
unity-of-opposites theorem
[
Bac02
]
.

\Box

7
Staircase Relations

For any binary relation
R
, the relations
R{\setminus}R
and
R{/}R
are preorders. That is, both are transitive and
reflexive. (If
R
has type
A{\sim}B
then
R{\setminus}R
has type
B{\sim}B
and
R{/}R
has type
A{\sim}A
.)
That relation
R
is a “staircase” relation means formally
that the preorder
R{\setminus}R
is linear
7
7

7

An
ordering
S
—of any sort— is said to be
linear
if
S\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptS^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
. Sometimes the word “total” is
used instead of linear. For example, Riguet
[
Rig51
]
uses the term “totalement
ordonnées”.
. For brevity, we denote the property of being a staircase relation by
\mathsf{SC}
. That is:

Definition 119

The predicate
\mathsf{SC}
on (binary) relations is defined by, for all
R
,

\mathsf{SC}{.}R\hskip 13.49945pt{\equiv}\hskip 13.49945ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994pt(R{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{=}\hskip 4.49982pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~.

A relation that satisfies the predicate
\mathsf{SC}
is called a
staircase
relation.

\Box

The pointwise formulation of the relation
R{\setminus}R
is

b0[\![{}R{\setminus}R]\!]{}b1\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957pt{\left\langle\forall{}a\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta[\![{}R]\!]{}b0\hskip 2.99988pt{:}\hskip 2.99988pta[\![{}R]\!]{}b1\right\rangle}~~.

In terms of the mental picture shown in fig.
1
, a vertical line through a
point
b
depicts the set of points
a
such that
a[\![{}R]\!]{}b
; a staircase relation is one such that the points of type
B

can be ordered in such a way that the preorder
R{\setminus}R
is depicted by the left-to-right ordering of points on
the
B
-axis, and the length of the vertical lines increases monotonically (although not always
strictly) as one proceeds from left to right in the diagram.

Various equivalent definitions of the predicate
\mathsf{SC}
are given below. Riguet
[
Rig51
]
defined a “relation
de Ferrers” to be a relation satisfying (
124
). The equivalence of (
121
) and (
122
)
corresponds in the mental picture of a staircase relation to the property that the
vertical lines being increasing in length is equivalent to the horizontal lines being decreasing in length.
(Cf. the statement of Riguet’s theorem quoted in the introduction.)

Lemma 120

The following are all equivalent formulations of
\mathsf{SC}{.}R
:

(121)

R{\setminus}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~,

(122)

R{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~,

(123)

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~,

(124)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR~~.

Proof
We prove first that (
122
) and (
124
) are equivalent:

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R

=

\{

complements
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}(R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})

=

\{

(
10
) with
R{,}S\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
(and
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
)
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 7.4997pt{\subseteq}\hskip 7.4997ptR{/}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\cup}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

(
13
) with
R{,}S\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{,}R
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\right]
with
S\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
and anti-symmetry
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

A symmetric argument establishes the equivalence of (
121
) and (
124
):

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R

=

\{

complements
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}({\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR)

=

\{

(
10
) with
S{,}T\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{,}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 7.4997pt{\subseteq}\hskip 7.4997ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\cup}\hskip 4.49982ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

(
14
) with
R{,}S\hskip 1.49994pt{:=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{,}R
(and
R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}})^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
)
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\right]
with
S\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
and anti-symmetry
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR{\setminus}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

Finally,

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

converse and complements
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 1.49994ptS\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\right]
with
S\hskip 2.99988pt{:=}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
and anti-symmetry
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}~~.

\Box

An example of a staircase relation predicted by lemma
125
is the at-most relation — on
natural numbers, integers, rational numbers or reals.

Two general methods for identifying examples of staircase relations are given in lemmas

125
and
126
.

Lemma 125

A linear preorder is a staircase relation. That is, for all (homogeneous)
R
,

\mathsf{SC}{.}R\hskip 10.49957pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~.

Proof
We have

R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 8.99963ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR

since

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\setminus}R{/}R

=

\{

factors
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR

\Leftarrow

\{

monontonicity and transitivity
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR

and

R{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR

=

\{

\left[\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptI{\setminus}R{/}I\hskip 2.99988pt\right]
~~~\}

R{\setminus}R{/}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptI{\setminus}R{/}I

\Leftarrow

\{

(anti)monotonicity
~~~\}

I\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

Also,

R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 10.49957pt{\equiv}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 4.49982pt{\wedge}\hskip 4.49982ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

(The converse of a preorder is a preorder.) So

\mathsf{SC}{.}R

=

\{

lemma
120
, in particular (
123
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}

=

\{

assumption:
R
is a preorder

(hence,
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
is a preorder and
R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\setminus}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{/}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
)

lemma
12
, in particular (
15
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}

=

\{

assumption:
R
is linear (i.e.
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
)
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

\Box

The second way of constructing a staircase relation is to reduce a linear preorder by eliminating its
reflexive part (making it so-called “strict”). For example, the less-than relation (on
natural numbers, integers, rational numbers or reals) is a staircase relation.
Formally, we have:

Lemma 126

For all (homogeneous)
R
,

\mathsf{SC}{.}R\hskip 10.49957pt{\Leftarrow}\hskip 10.49957ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\wedge}\hskip 5.99976ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~.

Proof

\mathsf{SC}{.}R

=

\{

(
123
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 5.99976pt{=}\hskip 5.99976pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 1.49994ptX\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 1.49994pt\right]
and antisymmetry
~~~\}

{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 2.99988pt{\cup}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Leftarrow

\{

assumption:
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}
, so
{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

monotonicity and transitivity
~~~\}

I\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

converse, factors and distributivity
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}I{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR

=

\{

supremum and monotonicity
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR

=

\{

assumption
~~~\}

\mathsf{true}~~.

\Box

Example 127

The less-than relations on the integers,
{<}_{{\mathsf{Z\hskip-4.0ptZ}}}
, on the rationals,
{<}_{{\mathsf{Q}}}
,
and on the reals,
{<}_{{\mathsf{I\!R}}}
,
are all staircase relations since in each case
<{\setminus}<
is the at-most relation,
\leq
.
See example
70
for details of the preorder in each case.
The less-than relation on the integers is a (linearly) block-ordered relation but the
less-than relation on the rationals and the less-than relation on the reals are not block-ordered. This is
because, as shown in example
70
, the less-than relations on the rationals and on the
reals both have empty diagonals.

\Box

That the less-than relation on the real
numbers is not block-ordered is a consequence of the fact that if
x\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994pty
the interval between
x
and
y
can
always be subdivided at will. (That is, it is always possible to find a real number
z
such that
x\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994ptz
and

z\hskip 1.49994pt{<}\hskip 1.49994pty
.) The same is also true of the rationals. Abstracting from the details of the less-than
relation, we get the following theorem. (Winter
[
Win04
]
proves a similar theorem. See section

8
for further discussion.)

Theorem 128

Suppose
R
is a homogeneous relation such that

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptI{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 8.99963pt{\wedge}\hskip 8.99963ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptI\hskip 1.49994pt{\cup}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{=}\hskip 2.99988pt{\top\kern-6.02773pt\top}~~.

Then
R
is a staircase relation and
\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.

It follows that any such relation is
not
block-ordered.

Proof
Lemma
126
proves that
R
is a staircase relation.

Comparing the above conditions on
R
with those in lemma
126
, the additions are the
non-emptiness property
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
, the “strictness” property
I{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
and the “subdivision” property

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
. (The less-than relation on real numbers has the subdivision property whereas the less-than
relation on the integers does not.) Applying lemma
129
(below), the subdivision and strictness
properties imply that
\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
. That
R
is not block-ordered follows from theorem
114
and the
assumption that
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\neq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}
.

\Box

The lemma used to prove theorem
128
is the following:

Lemma 129

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 2.99988pt{\Rightarrow}\hskip 2.99988pt(\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 2.99988pt{\equiv}\hskip 2.99988ptI{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot})~~.

Proof

R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\Rightarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

I{\cap}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\subseteq}\hskip 5.99976ptI\hskip 4.49982pt{\cap}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\Rightarrow

\{

modular law
~~~\}

I{\cap}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R

=

\{

assumption:
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
~~~\}

I{\cap}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}(R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}){\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R

=

\{

complements
~~~\}

I{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

I\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994ptI^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}
, converse and shunting
~~~\}

I\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

\Rightarrow

\{

monotonicity
~~~\}

R{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

\Rightarrow

\{

assumption:
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
and transitivity
~~~\}

R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR~~.

That is,

(130)

R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R\hskip 5.99976pt{\Rightarrow}\hskip 5.99976pt(R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR\hskip 5.99976pt{\equiv}\hskip 5.99976ptI{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot})~~.

So

\Delta{}R\hskip 1.49994pt{=}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

\left[\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptX\hskip 1.49994pt\right]
and antisymmetry, definition of
\Delta{}R
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\cap}\hskip 2.99988pt(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}

=

\{

shunting
~~~\}

R\hskip 2.99988pt{\subseteq}\hskip 2.99988pt{\neg}(R{\setminus}R{/}R)^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}

=

\{

(
11
)
~~~\}

R\hskip 4.49982pt{\subseteq}\hskip 4.49982ptR\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\neg}R^{\mbox{\raisebox{1.42262pt}{$\scriptscriptstyle\cup$}}}\hskip 1.49994pt{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}\hskip 1.49994ptR

=

\{

assumption:
R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994ptR{\mbox{\raisebox{0.86108pt}{$\scriptstyle\circ$}}}R
, (
130
)
~~~\}

I{\cap}R\hskip 1.49994pt{\subseteq}\hskip 1.49994pt{\bot\kern-6.02773pt\bot}~~.

\Box

8
Conclusion

The primary novel contribution of this paper is the combination of theorems
78
and

117
: essentially, a block-ordered relation is a relation whose core is a provisional
ordering. The discovery of these theorems was inspired by Riguet’s suggestion of an
“analogie frappante” linking the notion of a “relation de Ferrers” and the (difunctional) “différence”
of a relation. Theorem
114
is a precise statement of an “analogie” linking the notions of a
block-ordered relation and the diagonal of a relation.

A secondary, but nevertheless important, contribution of this paper is our (almost) exclusive use of the
properties of factors of a relation, particularly with respect to formulating and reasoning about the
notion of the diagonal of a relation (Riguet’s “différence”), as opposed to Riguet’s use of nested
complements. Indeed, our only use of complements is in section
7
where we
formulated the notion of a staircase relation —in terms of factors— and showed its equivalence to
Riguet’s notion of a “relation de Ferrers” —which he formulated in terms of nested complements— .

Our motivation for including section
7
is partly to give proper credit to Riguet’s
contribution but also to rectify misleading/incorrect statements
in the extant literature
8
8

8

See
[
Bac21
]
, in
particular the concluding section, for references to the relevant literature.
. Specifically, the claim that
a “relation de Ferrers … can be rewritten in staircase block form”
[
SS93
, Definition 4.4.11]

is, at best, confused: as shown in example
127
, the less-than relation on real numbers is a
staircase relation but not block-ordered. The lesson to be learnt is, in our view, that mental pictures, such
as the one of a staircase relation shown in fig.
1
and informal natural-language statements,
can never be relied on. Ultimately, it is vital that
informal notions are formalised and the properties of the formal notions are explored in detail in order
to confirm that they do indeed conform to their intended meaning.
